The AI boom could shift diesel generators from backup to everyday use

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Last week’s election results demand community-friendly data centers. Overlooking the role of diesel backup could create more pollution and noise for residents, write experts from Inlyte Energy.

Antonio Baclig is CEO of Inlyte Energy, which develops iron-sodium battery technology. Ben Kaun is Inlyte chief commercial officer and Jon Wellinghoff is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and a member of Inlyte’s advisory board.

In October, during what is supposed to be a slack season for the grid, when generators are taken offline for maintenance, a spell of unseasonable fall warmth prompted the U.S. Department of Energy, at PJM Interconnection’s request, to issue an emergency order to avoid blackouts. The event is yet another indication of the strain on grids from demand growth from data centers.

But the Oct. 13-16 event also reveals a crutch that is increasingly being leaned on during grid stress, which may be an unwelcome surprise for communities living next to data centers: the diesel generators that are installed for emergency backup. The DOE’s 202(c) order, consistent with similar orders begun this year around Winter Storm Fern, authorized PJM to direct backup generation at large loads such as data centers to run. Earlier this year, on July 2, as an intense summer heat dome settled over the Mid-Atlantic, PJM recorded its highest-ever unrestricted demand, reaching 168 GW. In Northern Virginia’s Data Center Alley, utility executives said they came within minutes of needing to call on the vast fleets of backup generators installed at data centers to keep the system supplied.

This is a remarkable development. For decades, those backup generators had a simple job: If the grid fails, keep the data center running. Now, as electricity demand grows faster than it has in decades and extreme weather increasingly tests the grid, the generators themselves are beginning to look like a potential source of grid capacity.

That may seem expedient. But this development is happening at the same time as the public backlash against data centers, seen in last week’s mid-term election results, is prompting states to impose requirements for data centers — in September alone, new rules on data centers were signed in Virginia, Massachusetts, Maryland and California. If policymakers and regulators do not pay attention to what is likely to happen during periods of grid strain, increased reliance on diesel may end up being the default for the U.S. grid.

The existence of huge fleets of behind-the-meter diesel generators is a new development brought on by the AI boom. A modern hyperscale data center is no longer simply another large electricity customer. Its electrical infrastructure can resemble that of a small city, with substations, private distribution networks, sophisticated power electronics and enormous amounts of backup generation. Because even a momentary interruption can be extraordinarily costly, data centers install enough backup capacity to keep operating through a major grid failure.

Diesel generators have been the default because they are proven, reliable and relatively compact. Diesel fuel is also extraordinarily energy-dense and can be stored on site. But the generators were designed to sit idle most of the time and operate only during emergencies.

Rapid load growth is changing that equation. New generation and transmission take years to permit and build, while enormous new data center loads can arrive much faster. During periods when the grid is short of capacity, thousands of idle diesel generators can therefore look like an irresistible resource.

Federal emergency authority has already been used to make such backup generation available during periods of severe grid stress. In January, DOE authorized grid operators to tap as much as 35 GW of idle backup generation at large facilities. Similar authority has been invoked 13 times this year, seven of those in PJM.

The danger is that an emergency exception becomes a routine operating model. Once policymakers and utilities begin counting on data-center diesel generators as a source of capacity, there will be less incentive to develop cleaner resources that can provide the same reliability services.

This extracts a local price. Diesel generators produce nitrous oxides, particulate matter and other pollutants. A study of Northern Virginia data centers found that operating their diesel generators up to permitted limits would increase the region’s total nitrous oxide emissions by 45%. The study also found that the census blocks most exposed to those emissions tend to have lower household incomes than other census blocks.

The noise is also disruptive. During a previous electrical emergency in Loudoun County, residents described the sound of data-center generators operating continuously as being like airplanes landing for more than 24 hours.

That may be tolerable as an emergency measure. It is a poor model for the everyday infrastructure of the electric grid.

Virginia is starting to ask the question: its order directs environmental regulators to review the cumulative impact of data center backup generation and to evaluate alternatives including on and offsite battery storage, load flexibility, fuel cells, and other non-diesel options. But will state and federal regulators, and data center companies themselves, take the next steps to prevent diesel generators from being the default solution?

This question is particularly important because data centers are being asked to become more flexible participants in the grid. Recent regulatory initiatives are creating pathways for large new loads to interconnect more quickly if they agree to reduce their demand when the system is short of capacity.

A data center cannot simply turn itself off. However, that does not mean it cannot be flexible. Many strategies are being developed to make data centers appear flexible to the grid, including throttling low-priority compute, shifting compute to other regions, and putting flexible resources such as batteries at the data center site, or nearby, connected front-of-the-meter or behind-the-meter at businesses and homes.

Batteries can reduce the data center’s demand on the grid during periods of extreme system stress. They can absorb electricity when supply is abundant and return it when electricity is scarce.

However, the lithium-ion batteries being installed today have short durations, for example two or four hours. For any longer period of outage or grid stress — and local congestion can create longer periods of stress than the system-wide peaks — if the data center still needs to be curtailed by the grid, where is that power going to come from?

Today, the default is the backup diesel generator, or in some cases natural gas generators. Therefore, once the battery runs out, the data center that is providing a grid resource instantly switches from doing so in a clean way with a battery to doing so in a dirty way with fossil fuels.

It is clear that data center flexibility can use the grid more efficiently, providing faster access to power at lower societal cost. However, there are many details to be worked out. As policymakers and regulators formulate and implement new data center rules, they should look carefully at how data center flexibility is likely to be met and only allow methods that are consistent with the overall goals of states. If they do not, the expediency of diesel generators could result in it being used more often, creating more pollution and noise for local communities.

Looking to the future, the most secure way to ensure that on-site fossil fuel generators are not used for data center flexibility is to not have them there at all. Why not just replace the diesel backup entirely with batteries, as recommended by the Better Data Center Project? A common objection is that the batteries do not provide enough backup duration.

One alternative is simple: long-duration energy storage, either on-site or proximate within the potentially congested area. A storage system capable of providing 24, 48, or even 72 hours of backup could give a data center a high level of resilience, while at the same time allowing the facility to strengthen the grid productively on an everyday basis.

And long-duration storage does not necessarily mean installing more lithium-ion batteries. New battery chemistries, and other clean backup technologies, are emerging that use more abundant materials, are designed for stationary rather than transportation applications, and can potentially offer different combinations of safety, cost, footprint and duration.

For data centers, footprint is particularly important. Land around a hyperscale facility is scarce. Technologies that can safely pack long durations of stored energy into a relatively small area could make it possible to replace diesel backup without requiring any more land. Additionally, LDES could be placed for the benefit of the data center at other consumer locations such as commercial and industrial sites.

The immediate objective should not be to pick a single winning technology today. It should be to make sure that data-center developers and regulators do not assume that diesel generators are the only practical answer, and that they work to incentivize and develop alternative technologies. So far, LDES has been framed as a front-of-meter bulk storage solution, and state incentives have only rewarded behind-the-meter short duration storage. There should be a signal to the data center or other customers that the resilience contribution of behind-the-meter LDES is valued for bulk system reliability and the elimination of local emissions. This could take the form of allowing behind-the-meter LDES to be accredited towards system capacity and state procurement mandates while taking into account the full effective load carrying value of their durations, as well as modifying existing behind-the-meter rebate and demand response incentives to reward longer durations rather than capping them at shorter duration.

Across the country, residents are increasingly questioning the impacts of data centers: their electricity consumption, water use, noise, and local pollution. Some of those impacts may be unavoidable. But many are the consequence of choices about how these facilities are designed.

Virginia and other states are beginning to define what a community-friendly data center looks like. The industry should meet that standard by building data centers that are clean and quiet stewards of their communities from the start, rather than locking yesterday’s infrastructure into tomorrow’s grid.

TON Strategy earns $13.1 million from GRAM staking in Q3 as Telegram rolls out native wallet

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The company also repurchased $5.7 million of its shares and supported TON network upgrades as Telegram began rolling out its native Gram Wallet.

Nasdaq-listed TON Strategy Company (TONX) reported $13.1 million in third-quarter revenue, primarily from staking rewards on its GRAM treasury, compared with $15 million in the second quarter.

The company held approximately 235.1 million GRAM (formerly Toncoin) worth $348.5 million at the quarter's end, with 232.9 million GRAM deployed in staking and generating an annualized gross yield of approximately 16%.

The company also repurchased $5.7 million of its shares and supported TON network upgrades as Telegram began rolling out its native Gram Wallet.

TON Strategy posted a $24 million net loss, including a $27.2 million unrealized loss on its GRAM holdings, reversing an $82.8 million unrealized gain in the prior quarter. The firm said it made progress toward a more efficient cost structure following the wind-down of inherited VERB operations.

"GRAM is integrated into several existing Telegram services, including advertising payments and creator monetization, and TON supports blockchain-based ownership and transfer of Telegram usernames and gifts," CEO Kevin Wilson said in a release. "We believe expanded native wallet access can make GRAM easier to use for payments and transfers, while opening additional opportunities for consumer applications built on TON."

In a deal led by Kingsway Capital, the now-former Verb Technology Company raised about $560 million in August 2025 to rebrand into a treasury company for The Open Network's native token.

Earlier this year, TON was renamed to Gram, reviving the original branding outlined in Telegram's white paper.

"700K+ self-custodial wallets created on Telegram in the last 48 hours alone. Adoption spreads virally: a wallet activates the moment someone receives Grams. The crypto revolution is going strong," Telegram founder and CEO Pavel Durov posted to X on Monday.

TONX shares were up 2.4% in Tuesday's session, putting their year-to-date gains near 25%.

There are a lot of all-in-one soundbars out there these days, but separates can still make the difference for a home entertainment setup, and they don't always have to cost so much more. Right now, AO has a massive £700 off the LG US80TR, reducing the price to just £399.

This is a full 5.1.3-channel package with real rear satellite speakers and a wireless subwoofer. Many of them come as a single piece and use processing to try to replicate the effect of surround sound. The LG US80TR 5.1.3 does things properly with physical rear speakers and subwoofer for more immersive sound while still keeping clutter to a minimum.

It comprises the main soundbar, wireless rear satellites and a dedicated subwoofer. With an output of 580W, it can handle quiet dialogue as well as loud sound effects and soundtracks with nuance and sensitivity. The drivers bounce sound off your ceiling to create overhead effects like rain, while the dedicated center channel puts dialogue at the fore where you want it and the subwoofer delivers a punch at the low end, also serving for music and gaming.

Anthropic has launched its IPO roadshow, with 825 million shares proposed at $70 to $78 each.

The company could reach a fully diluted valuation of approximately $1.4 trillion.

A separate $26 billion private placement includes Google, Nvidia and AMD as cornerstone investors.

Anthropic is heading toward Wall Street with a hefty price tag and an even bigger question: how much appetite does the market still have for AI? The Anthropic IPO roadshow has officially begun, with shares expected to price between $70 and $78.

The proposed offering covers 825 million Class A shares. Anthropic is selling 801,351,354 shares, while existing stockholders are offering another 23,648,646. The company also expects to give underwriters a 30-day option for up to 123.75 million additional shares.

At the top of the proposed range, Anthropic’s fully diluted valuation would reach approximately $1.4 trillion. Strip out options and restricted stock, however, and its market value comes in around $1 trillion. That’s quite a difference, depending on which number investors choose to focus on.

The IPO isn’t the only capital event on the table. Anthropic is also set to raise $26 billion through a separate private placement involving cornerstone investors, including Google, Nvidia and even AMD among the list.

The listing arrives as higher borrowing costs, geopolitical turmoil and elevated bond yields weigh on the IPO market. That makes this offering a significant test of investor confidence in the AI boom.

Anthropic has applied to list on the Nasdaq Global Select Market under the ticker ANT. But the registration statement filed with the SEC has not yet become effective, meaning shares cannot legally be sold before that happens.

With SpaceX having raised $75 billion at a nearly $1.75 trillion valuation, comparisons are inevitable. The Anthropic IPO now has to prove that public-market investors are still willing to pay a premium for AI growth.

Why Aging Stars Suffer A Sudden Luminosity Dip

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

From a naked eye perspective, stars are stable and predictable. Not much goes on but shining. The Sun itself is fairly plane and nondescript and will shine relatively steadily for billions of years. It's considered a low-mass star compared to the most massive stars, which are rarer and contain many times its mass.

Powerful telescopes bring millions of stars of different types and ages into view. Astrophysicists and astronomers watch the Sun and its low-mass brethren, and note changes in their shining, just as they do all types of stars. Their observations reveal the changes stars experience during their long lifetimes.

As low-mass stars like the Sun exhaust their hydrogen supplies and evolve, they become Red Giant Branch (RGB) stars. This is not only theoretical, it's an observational conclusion. RGB stars are easy to observe and study for two reasons: they're bright, and they're numerous. Arcturus, Aldebaran, and Pollus are all bright, naked-eye stars and are all RGB stars. (Well-known Betelgeuse is a Red Supergiant, not quite the same.)

As RGB stars age and cool, they swell in size and become more luminous. It's a pretty steady change. But for some reason, there's a brief and abrupt dip in their brightness known as the RGB luminosity bump. The bump is called that because of how it appears in the colourâmagnitude diagrams of stellar clusters.

Astrophysicists know why the bump ends. But what initiates the bump is still unexplained. Astrophysicists don't like this gap in their knowledge and they've been trying to fill it in.

New research in The Astrophysical Journal Letters has an explanation for the bump. It's titled "The Role of Specific Entropy in the Onset of the Luminosity Bump," and the sole author is Saskia Hekker. Hekker is a Professor of Theoretical Astrophysics and the Group Leader of the Theory and Observations of Stars Group at the Heidelberg Institute for Theoretical Studies.

"During their evolution, stars follow a distinct path in luminosityâtemperature space. Low-mass stars that have exhausted hydrogen in their core follow the so-called red-giant branch (RGB), which is predominantly in the direction of increasing luminosity and decreasing surface temperature," Hekker explains. "The luminosity bump is a temporary decrease in luminosity against this otherwise increasing trend."

The inset grey box is the Hertzsprung-Russel diagram for a 1 solar mass star progressing from the subgiant branch to the tip of the Red Giant Branch. The tiny grey box inside it is magnified in the larger part of the image, highlighting the RGB luminosity bump. Image Credit: S. Hekker 2026 ApJL 1008 L61

The bump is understood theoretically and also confirmed with observations. But there's one discrepancy between theory and observation. In models, the bump appears at higher luminosities than the observed bump, meaning it appears later. "To understand why the bump appears later in the models than in observations, the physics of the bump needs to be unravelled," Hekker writes.

Stars, including our Sun, are layered, and RGB stars are no exception. But unlike the Sun, RGB stars have exhausted the hydrogen in their cores, and most of what's left is in their envelopes. Since the core has exhausted its hydrogen by fusing it into helium, the core is inert helium. An RGB's fusion occurs in a thin shell. In both these regions, energy is transported via radiation.

Above this radiative region is a convective outer envelope.

As an RGB star ascends the branch, the core contracts and the surface expands. Since the core is inert helium, it exerts no outward pressure and is forced inward. "This causes the deepest layer of the convective envelope to move inwards, reaching layers that have changed their chemical composition due to (burning) processes earlier in the evolution of the star," Hekker explains.

This is when dredge-up occurs, as deeper layers are forced into the convective zone. Since the convective zone is convective, the material from the radiative zone mixes into the convective zone, changing its composition.

Now there's a situation where there's a shifting boundary deep inside the star called the Base of the Convective Zone (BCZ). It's organized around a thermodynamic property called specific entropy. It basically measures how disordered the gas is at the BCZ. As the BCZ moves deeper inside the star, the entropy difference across the boundary shrinks. Past a certain point, entropy across the boundary layer drops past a critical value, and that triggers the luminosity bump.

This diagram shows the interior of an RGB star (not to scale). An inert helium core is in the center, and it's contained in a thin hydrogen-burning shell. Above that is a radiative zone separated from the star's convective envelope by the base of the convective zone (BCZ). Earlier dredge-up and mixing created this boundary between helium-enriched and hydrogen-rich gas. This work ties the RGB luminosity bump to this boundary. The author argues that the onset of the bump occurs when the entropy across this boundary crosses a critical value. (Image Generated with AI)

Previous explanations for the bump rested on mechanics. In those explanations, the thin hydrogen-burning shell reaches the boundary and something inside the star changes, triggering the bump. In this explanation, the bump happens when an entropy threshold is crossed inside the star's thermodynamics. It's not simply that layers meet.

We can't see inside stars. Astrophysicists use physics to try to understand what's going on there, and they test those physics against the only part of the star they can really see, the outside. It's a constant back and forth to refine theories until they match observations.

âThis succinct explanation of the onset of the bump, is the first quantitative description proposed so far,â said Hekker.

This new work will make its way into the larger body of astrophysical theory.

"This viable description of the mechanism behind the onset of the bump provides crucial and new insights for the exploitation of the bump as an anchor point in stellar evolution theory," Hekker writes. "Such added knowledge of the onset of the bump could potentially be used to make more informed choices for changes to the physics in stellar models that go beyond calibration of the overshoot, to place the bump in stellar evolution models at the correct luminosity while at the same time aligning them with measured surface abundances."

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

Unlike its biggest competitor, OpenAI, Anthropic doesn't appear to be backing away from its IPO.

Anthropic, the AI giant behind the Claude chatbot, is preparing for an IPO roadshow, according to an announcement from the company. An IPO roadshow is basically a series of sales presentations to potential investors in order to gauge interest and set a final share price.

Interestingly, Anthropic is looking to go public at a $1.4 trillion valuation. This is lower than investor expectations, which reportedly reached $2 trillion just two months ago.

Anthropic's decision to go forward with an IPO is in sharp contrast to the approach of its largest competitor, OpenAI, which has largely shied away from doing so anytime in the near future. Anthropic beat OpenAI to an IPO filing earlier this summer when both AI companies were still pursuing a public stock offering. Since then, OpenAI has put its IPO plans on hold, citing concerns around AI safety. Anthropic's IPO will be a first among the big AI companies, and it comes as investors' faith in the AI boom seems to be waning.

One major issue for investors is that the big players in the AI industry are simply unprofitable. Anthropic's prospectus, which lays out its finances for potential investors, says that the company brought in $4.6 billion in revenue last year and posted a $42 billion loss. Former Manchester United boss Jose Mourinho hailed Dalot as the ‘best young full-back in Europe’ at the time but the Portugal international has attracted much criticism during his time at Old Trafford.

Dalot has endured a particularly poor couple of weeks, struggling in United’s Champions League win over Roma and Sunday’s 4-4 draw with Aston Villa in the Premier League.

The 27-year-old was branded ‘atrocious’ after the Roma clash and was booed as he was subbed off by Michael Carrick against Villa.

How Nick Cannon Really Feels About Mariah Carey Prenup

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Ten years after Nick Cannon and Mariah Carey finalized their divorce, the  America’s Got Talent alum, who was married to the singer from 2008 to 2016, revealed the details of their prenup.

Boy, if you wanna know whether Nick Cannon and Mariah Carey had a prenup—he’ll tell you.

Ten years after the former couple—who share 15-year-old twins Moroccan and Monroe—finalized their divorce, the former America’s Got Talent host confirmed they “absolutely” signed a prenuptial agreement before tying the knot in 2008.

“Mariah Carey, her entity, her global conglomerate, is gonna make sure she is protected,” Cannon explained during a Nov. 9 appearance on the On Par with Maury Povich podcast. “Had no problem with it whatsoever. And when it was all said and done, I think it protected both of us.”

Although their divorce wasn’t finalized for two years following their 2014 breakup, the 46-year-old—dad to 10 additional children with five different women—said the process felt “easy.”

Nevertheless, it’s not a process Cannon sees himself going through again, as he doesn’t see himself ever getting remarried.

“I think because of some of the natures of, like, unmarrying are not good,” he continued. “And it’s just the process—you don’t realize it takes so long and how draining it is.”

And while divorce “didn’t affect” his friendship with Carey, he saw how divorce creates pressure points in relationships.

“It really could have affected how we handled one another as friends,” he admitted. “I saw the traps of how this could have turned really bad if we didn’t step in and really engage as a family.”

For Cannon, he considered the friendliness that exists between he and Carey an exception when it comes to divorce.

“We handled it so well,” he added. “We handled it out of love. Never heard me say a bad thing about her. Never heard her say a bad thing about me. And we just kind of made it about the kids.”

Meanwhile, Cannon—who is currently in a non-monogamous relationship with Bre Tiesi and Abby De La Rosa—previously went so far to admit that he and Carey might have another shot at romance in the future.

“We belong together,” he quipped to E! News in 2024, going on to say that he would “be stupid” not to get back together with Carey.

As for why the fantasy hasn’t turned into reality? Cannon acknowledged, “She don’t want me.”

Borussia Dortmund receive encouraging news on Giannis Konstantelias’ injury recovery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Borussia Dortmund attacking midfielder Giannis Konstantelias is reportedly making encouraging progress in his recovery from a serious knee injury, raising hopes that the Greek international could return to action earlier than initially anticipated. According to BILD, the 23-year-old is progressing ahead of schedule in his rehabilitation from the anterior cruciate ligament tear he suffered during Dortmund’s Bundesliga opener against Hamburger SV in August.

Konstantelias has been working intensively on his recovery following successful surgery in September, reportedly completing rehabilitation sessions at least five days per week. The German tabloid reports, citing sources within Dortmund, that the midfielder is ahead of his original rehabilitation schedule. A comeback as early as February or March 2027 is now being discussed.

Borussia Dortmund have yet to establish an official timetable for his comeback.

Konstantelias made an immediate impression following his arrival, scoring his first Bundesliga goal in Dortmund’s 2-0 opening-day victory over Hamburg. The 23-year-old produced an outstanding first-half display, earning an eight in our Get German Football News player ratings before suffering the injury shortly after the interval.

The initial fears of a serious knee injury were subsequently confirmed, leaving head coach Niko Kovac without one of his most promising summer acquisitions. The setback also prompted Dortmund to strengthen their attacking options, with Arsenal youngster Ethan Nwaneri arriving on loan to provide additional cover.

A potential return during the closing months of the campaign would provide Kovac with another creative option as Dortmund approach the decisive stages of the Bundesliga season.

Webb reveals the hidden activity inside a turbulent stellar nursery

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Webb peers through thick cosmic dust in NGC 7129, revealing hidden young stars, protostars, glowing gas, and powerful outflows.

Today’s Image of the Day from the European Space Agency reveals a stellar nursery where young stars are changing the clouds that gave birth to them.

The James Webb Space Telescope captured NGC 7129, a star-forming region about 3,300 light-years from Earth.

Thick clouds of gas and dust obscure much of the activity at visible wavelengths.

Webb’s infrared vision can penetrate that material, bringing previously hidden stars and structures into view.

The result shows much more than a collection of newborn stars. NGC 7129 contains objects at several stages of early stellar development.

Some are still gathering material. Others have moved closer to the long, stable period of life that includes stars such as our Sun.

Stars do not all mature at the same rate. More massive stars generally form and evolve faster than smaller ones.

That difference allows astronomers to study several chapters of stellar development within the same region.

The brightest central star in this scene is LkH(alpha) 234. It has about five to eight times the mass of the Sun and produces the image’s most noticeable diffraction pattern.

It is a pre-main-sequence star, meaning it has largely finished collecting mass but has not yet begun the sustained hydrogen fusion that will define its main-sequence life.

At this stage, gravity continues to pull the star inward. As it contracts, its interior grows hotter.

Eventually, conditions in the core will become intense enough for hydrogen fusion to begin.

This transition matters because a young star does not develop quietly.

Even before reaching the main sequence, it can send powerful streams of material into the gas around it.

Those outflows can reshape a stellar nursery across surprisingly large distances.

The clearest evidence appears to the left of LkH(alpha) 234. A golden cavity stretches roughly 3.5 light-years across.

Outflows produced during an earlier stage of the star’s development carved into the surrounding molecular cloud, which consists largely of hydrogen.

Radiation and outflowing material from the star energize the gas and make it glow.

Some gas gets swept away, but the process does not simply shut down star formation.

Other material becomes compressed. Dense pockets can then provide the conditions needed for additional stars to develop.

Several young stars are visible inside the cavity. Some are also pre-main-sequence stars, and they produce stellar winds of their own.

As these winds push into surrounding gas, they create curved structures called bow shocks.

The stars also create a hot environment beside colder, denser molecular material. The boundary between these environments is known as a photodissociation region.

Energetic radiation breaks hydrogen molecules apart into individual atoms there.

The process changes the chemistry, heating and cooling of the cloud and helps determine how it evolves.

Molecular clouds are the raw material for star formation, but the stars born inside them gradually help destroy them.

Radiation, winds, and outflows can disperse gas or alter its chemistry.

Over millions of years, those effects can erode the cloud and change where new stars are able to form.

NGC 7129 gives astronomers a particularly useful view of that process because its stars have reached different developmental stages.

Their combined activity shows how star formation can both disrupt a cloud and compress parts of it into conditions favorable for further stellar growth.

The right side of Webb’s image tells an earlier part of the story.

Clumpy red material conceals protostars that are younger than the pre-main-sequence stars visible elsewhere in the region.

Protostars form after cold molecular gas and dust collapse and fragment into dense concentrations.

They continue gaining mass from surrounding material.

At the same time, they can eject narrow, extremely hot outflows that slam into the dense matter around them.

Those collisions produce shocks within the gray material surrounding the protostars.

They help create the textured appearance seen by Webb and contribute to the region’s red glow.

Several protostars are active at once, so their outflows overlap from Earth’s viewpoint. That makes this side of NGC 7129 look especially tangled.

Yet the apparent disorder contains valuable information about stars that would otherwise remain hidden behind dust.

Infrared observations give astronomers access to these deeply embedded objects before they emerge from their birth clouds.

In NGC 7129, Webb captures stars at different points along that path while also revealing how strongly they affect their surroundings.

The nursery will continue changing as its young stars mature.

Gas will disperse, dense pockets may collapse, and some newly formed stars will eventually settle into the hydrogen-fusing stage that powers the Sun today.

NFL trade deadline 2026: Denver Broncos latest news and rumors

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The NFL trade deadline is Tuesday, November 10th at 2 p.m. MT, and our Denver Broncos could be an active team prior to the deadline. They have been widely connected to Colts star running back Jonathan Taylor in the days leading up to the deadline, but according to 9NEWS Denver's Mike Klis, the team could explore adding a weapon or two to their offense. He mentions running back, tight end, and wide receiver as the positions that they could add.

Looking at the Broncos' needs heading into the deadline, the most glaring one is running back. Now, the emergence of rookie running back Jonah Coleman helps, but the Broncos still need more from the position. RJ Harvey and Tyler Badie are on the injured reserve, and veteran JK Dobbins has been struggling all season. So, adding another back to pair with Coleman would make sense.

The question is, will they go big and add a Jonathan Taylor or Breece Hall? Or make a smaller move for someone like Tony Pollard or someone else?

Klis mentions wide receiver, and while it may not be the most pressing need right now, it could be in a few months. Jaylen Waddle has not performed as expected thus far; Courtland Sutton will be 32 next season and has a large cap hit(Also restructured his deal and could be off the team next season); Pat Bryant has been very good, but has been on the IR; Marvin Mims is a pending free agent, while Troy Franklin is buried on the depth chart. Could the Broncos be looking to add a wide receiver who could contribute to this team this season and moving forward? This doesn't seem as likely, but I wouldn't rule it out if the right deal comes their way.

As for tight end, I don't know. The only name that could move the needle and that might be available is Dallas Goedert, but is that even worth it? He would certainly improve that room, but the Broncos really do not utilize their tight ends in the passing game. Evan Engram has had a disappointing year, and blocking specialist Nate Adkins leads the group (and, funny enough, the team) in TD receptions with 3, but he only has 6 total catches thus far.

We'll be tracking all the Broncos news and rumors throughout the weekend and right up to the deadline. It's supposed to be an active deadline, so we'll see if our Denver Broncos make any splashes or not in the coming days.

Scientists tracked millions of phones to uncover patterns in carbon emissions

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Scientists used anonymized cell phone mobility data to estimate carbon emissions, testing the approach across four countries.

When COVID-19 lockdowns froze the planet in early 2020, carbon emissions plummeted almost overnight.

The problem? The instruments tracking those emissions couldn’t keep up.

Conventional inventories lag reality by months. Satellite inversions come with their own trade-offs between resolution and coverage.

A new study explores another approach: using people’s movements to help estimate carbon emissions.

The research was led by Mengning Wang and Xin Lu from China’s National University of Defense Technology.

Collaborators included Xiaoling Zhang from the University of Hong Kong and scholars from various international institutions.

The researchers turned anonymized cell phone signals from 318 million Chinese mobile users into what amounts to a near-real-time carbon meter.

They tested the framework across four countries.

Commuting, deliveries, business trips, and holiday travel often generate carbon emissions.

The researchers reasoned that if so many carbon-producing processes involve physically moving people and goods, then the movement itself might serve as a fast measuring stick.

They took anonymized signaling data from China Unicom and converted it into daily intercity mobility networks.

Each city became a node and each flow of people between cities became an edge.

From these networks they extracted structural features – how central a city is, how tightly clustered its connections are, how many links it maintains – and combined those with population and geographic data.

Then they trained machine learning models against the GRACED dataset, which tracks daily CO2 emissions across seven sectors: power, industry, residential consumption, ground transport, domestic aviation, international aviation, and shipping.

The window researchers studied was January and February 2020. It’s hard to imagine a harder test.

Spring Festival travel sent 68.74 million daily intercity trips surging through the network. Then lockdowns hit.

Mobility crashed by 71.3%. Multi-source carbon emissions dropped from an average of roughly 356.5 million kilograms of carbon per day to a minimum of 209.6 million – a 41.2% collapse. The whole thing happened in weeks.

And yet the correlation between daily national mobility and CO2 emissions held firm at r = 0.89. At the city level, it was a more modest but still significant r = 0.49.

The best-performing model – LightGBM, a tree-based ensemble – achieved R² values of 1.00 for multi-source emissions, 0.99 for domestic aviation, and 0.98 for ground transportation across 366 cities and 60 days.

Those are extraordinary numbers for something as messy as urban carbon output.

The real surprise came when the framework crossed borders.

Using open-source mobility datasets, the team applied the same approach to Italy, the continental United States, and Mexico.

The model reached R² values of 0.91, 0.95, and 0.99 respectively against the GRACED inventory.

Tested against a completely independent dataset – ODIAC, which derives emissions from nighttime light imagery and power-plant data at 1 km resolution – it still scored between 0.90 and 0.99.

Four countries, two independent benchmarks, consistent accuracy.

Something unexpected emerged from the mobility data. The Louvain community detection algorithm carved China’s 366 cities into 11 functional clusters.

These are groupings defined not by provincial borders but by how people actually move between them. Beijing-Tianjin-Hebei formed one cluster, pumping out 62.75 million kilograms of carbon daily.

The Yangtze River Delta formed another, at 49.71 million.

These clusters aren’t just descriptive. More central, more connected clusters emit more carbon.

But here’s the policy hook. China’s existing climate governance tends to target a handful of major emitting cities while overlooking smaller ones that sit in structurally critical positions within the network.

The mobility-defined clusters offer something different: a template for coordinated regional decarbonization that matches how economies actually function rather than how administrative maps happen to be drawn.

The authors aren’t overselling this. They call mobility a “partial but observable proxy” – a complement to inventories, not a replacement.

The Chinese analysis covers a single extraordinary two-month window. The data captures intercity movement, not what happens within a city.

Mobility alone may not fully capture emissions changes driven by seasonal energy demand, holiday schedules, or weekly cycles.

There are subtler problems too. Cellular signaling data inherits all the biases of telecom coverage – regional disparities, operator market share, sampling artifacts.

And the fundamental link between mobility and emissions could shift as remote work spreads, transport electrifies, and travel patterns reshape.

A correlation forged during the most dramatic behavioral disruption in modern history might not hold the same way during a quiet Tuesday in 2030.

Still, the core appeal is hard to argue with. Standard emissions inventories are accurate but glacially slow.

With timely access to mobility data, the framework could provide frequently updated emissions estimates.

If it scales, climate policymakers would gain a behavior-informed reading of the present rather than a months-old accounting of the past.

The team calls for fusing their mobility approach with remote sensing, energy structure data, and meteorological variables.

The ambition is to make carbon monitoring something closer to weather forecasting: imperfect, always updating, but fast enough to actually be useful.

The study is published in the journal The Innovation.

Stock Market Midday, Nov. 10: Stocks Slide on Hot Inflation Data

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Growth and tech shares are leading the intraday slide as traders weigh a hotter-than-expected inflation report today, Nov. 10, 2026.

As of 11:41 AM ET, the Nasdaq Composite (^IXIC -1.19%) is down 1.09% to 24,987, the S&P 500 (^GSPC -0.83%) has fallen 0.79% to 7,490, and the Dow Jones Industrial Average (^DJI -0.61%) is trading 0.70% lower at 52,039 as persistent inflation data weighs on sentiment.

Gold is trading down 2.82% to $4,023.75, while the 10-Year Treasury yield rose 0.12% to 5.02%. Energy stocks were the only real gainers this morning as oil prices fell on hopes that the conflict in Iran may come to an end. Basic materials saw the biggest drop, followed by tech stocks.

Sanmina shares are up almost 4% on robust fiscal 2027 guidance. The stock surged in premarket trading before retreating this morning. Sally Beauty also jumped following a fourth-quarter earnings beat. Conversely, Rocket Lab performance is muted following mixed earnings. Nvidia remains under pressure as the fallout from the board's investigation into CEO Jensen Huang's conduct.

Yesterday's optimism was short-lived as October inflation data came in hotter than expected today, fueling fears that the Federal Reserve might not only increase interest rates in December, but also hike them again in 2027. Comments from Fed officials added to investor concern, and Treasury yields climbed.

One impact of high interest rates is to make borrowing more expensive, which can eat into corporate profits, pressuring stocks, particularly growth stocks. That is particularly important right now as some AI firms have taken on significant debt to fund infrastructure. Riskier assets are also less appealing when safer investments generate solid returns.

Investors will be monitoring CoreWeave's results after the bell, when its AI spending and financing will be under scrutiny.

To Penny, or Not to Penny?

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Few shoes are as essential in the modern menswear landscape as the classic leather loafer. Whether you’re dressing up or dressing down, it is the “the Swiss Army Knife of shoes,” an any-occasion solution to many footwear conundrums. But within the broader loafer genus, one particular species—the penny loafer—includes a divisive design feature: a small, coin-shaped slot in the leather strap “saddle” across the vamp of the shoe.

As the lore goes, Ivy Style pioneers and sock-hopping teens in the mid-20th century liked to store a penny in their shoes as “payphone change.” But now that payphones are a thing of the misty-eyed past and the American one-cent penny is no longer minted for circulation, is it still cool to put a penny (or any other coin, or coin-shaped object) in your penny loafers? What is the line between timeless and passé?

We invited two of GQ’s most well-heeled staffers, associate grooming and wellness editor Tyler Chin and senior style commerce writer Gerald Ortiz, to ponder the pros and cons of the penny loafer.

Mahershala Ali, an ostensible penny proponent, in London on October 19.

Tyler Chin, associate grooming and wellness editor: Sure! I’ve seen so many guys wearing loafers these days, and yet still haven’t seen any of them harking back to the shoe’s origins with a penny in them. Maybe I’m not looking hard enough or I’m not in circles where they are doing that, but it’s an inoffensive and easy way to accessorize the shoe. Plus, I had an English teacher in high school who did it, and while she wasn’t particularly cool, I did think the sartorial choice was neat.

Gerald Ortiz, senior style commerce writer: No. I feel like guys who put pennies in their loafers are either try-hards, or just learned about that particular piece of prep trivia. The only acceptable instance to wear a penny in your loafers is if you’re really about that prep/Ivy style, like Preppy Pete, or the guys who frequent Alfargo’s Market (shout out).

TC: I think the penny would be the most boring and obvious choice. If a quarter could fit in there, I think it’d be so funny if you were at a vending machine and short a few cents and settled up with the coin in your shoe. One time, the button on my pants fell off on the street and I lost it, but imagine I had a spare button in my shoe? I could reattach it right there and then! In the hypothetical I’d also need to be toting around a sewing kit, but hey, I’d love to think I could be MacGyver in that scenario.

GO: It doesn’t have to be anything at all. But if you’re going to do it, I don’t see why it couldn’t be another coin or object. It could be a Chuck E. Cheese token. Whatever you put in the slot, it would still be wack.

TC: I just got back from a trip to Tokyo, and I had a lot of leftover yen coins. Two of them—the five and 50 yen—have a hole on them, which differentiates them from your boring American coin. I’d throw one of them in my shoe and be one of those people who never stops talking about their travel abroad.

GO: I saw on Instagram someone stick a hundred dollar bill into their loafer and that was funny for a second. Then I scrolled on to the next meme.

TC: I’m pretty sure a coin that is that close to the ground will surely have a patina before the end of the day. Also, I can’t remember the last time I’ve seen a shiny new coin—who carries loose change these days anyway?!

GO: You know, it might be kind of cool to see a penny completely covered in verdigris slotted into your loafers. But maybe the coin should match the sheen of your loafers. I dunno, just don’t do it and you won’t have to think about it.

TC: Everyone I know is very familiar with my love of penny loafers. Lately I’ve been wearing them with bootcut jeans that are just a little longer than usual, so they have a nice drape over the shoe. Top-wise, I’d go with a tucked-in button up, T-shirt, or sweater. Pretty much anything that would go well with sneakers would be equally suited for wearing with loafers. Which is nearly all outfits.

GO: Penniless or not, a pair of wide khakis with a clean-to-slight break and a striped button-up is a failsafe outfit. It wouldn’t be very inventive, but it would pass muster for most conceivable situations. Vintage straight-leg jeans with good fades, a fucked-up band tee, and a sportcoat is also a great look. But the best looks are always the unexpected pairings.

TC: It’s sort of like how guys will go on and on about having functional sleeve buttons on a suit jacket. Is there any situation where you’ll actually need to unbutton those or do you just leave one or two undone to “stunt” on your unimpressed bros? If you really want to put a coin in your shoe for the sake of accessorizing your footwear, then by all means get a shoe with a functioning penny slot and use it! But if you’re doing it just so you can say “Yeah, these joints are the real deal!” and use it as an excuse to talk about the history of the shoe and why yours are superior, then I’d rather you wear lace-ups.

GO: I think it’s fine, even preferable to have a faux penny slot. It’s like how jeans still come with the watch pocket. It’s purely vestigial at this point, but would feel incomplete without it.

TC: One of these days, I’ll be telling my great-great grandchildren about the origins of the penny loafer and they’ll be confused because they would’ve never heard or seen the one-cent coin before. I’ll whip one out of my shoe and instead of being impressed they’ll chalk it up to another one of Old Man Ty’s tales from his youth, and tuck me back into the nursing home they checked me into.

GO: Lol. I guess it’ll make the penny more covetable. But it’ll be a long time before the penny becomes that desirable. That said, I love this British brand Six 95 that’s designing cool pieces using coins, so I can absolutely see more labels farming pennies for their clothes and accessories.

TC: Men will break up anything other than a toxic relationship. Loafers did nothing wrong and sometimes it’s just the person wearing them that needs to hit the curb.

GO: It makes sense. It’s like any trend, really. Even if they’re as versatile as we’ve been propping them up to be, loafers reached an inflection point where, for some guys, they’re just not the vibe anymore. But I don’t think they’ll dip so low to be thought of as cringe or as an emblem of the 2020s. They’re as classic as it gets and they’ll go through their own ebbs and flows.

TC: A quarter to increase my net worth 25-fold.

Cool adaptations: How fish survive in the Antarctic Ocean

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

With water temperatures as low as -1.9°C (28.6°F), the Southern Ocean is a place of extreme living conditions. A new study led by Senckenberg researchers shows how fish have adapted to Antarctic temperatures. The researchers used the Antarctic spiny plunderfish (Harpagifer antarcticus) as a model organism and, for the first time, analyzed the maternal and paternal copies of its genome separately. This allowed them to uncover mechanisms that contribute to this group of fishes' adaptation to extreme cold.

Netflix’s New Historical Epic Gets a 117% Box Office Boost

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

It's that time of year again when the early favorites for next year's Oscars enter the theatrical circuit, hoping to boost their profile with a strong box office return. One of the biggest contenders for the Best Picture crown is A24's You Can See Everything, a three-hour documentary ready to become the first of its kind to earn a nomination in the Academy's most prestigious category.

Called "the year's most original movie" in a review from Collider's Samuel R. Murrian, Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim's You Can See Everything isn't just a critical smash. Thanks to positive word of mouth and its viral appeal, the documentary about the pre-incarceration days of the infamous Theranos founder, Elizabeth Holmes​​​​, has also proved a big hit at the box office. Last weekend, not only did it reach the $80 million mark in North America, but it also became the second-highest-grossing non-music documentary ever in the region.

But You Can See Everything wasn't the only Oscars favorite to score big at last weekend's box office. Javier Calvo and Javier Ambrossi's 155-minute epic La Bola Negra, which was called a "clear frontrunner for many categories at the Oscars" in Victoria Luxford's review for Collider, just received a 117% boost in ticket sales compared to the previous weekend. This is thanks to the theatrical net being cast wider, as La Bola Negra was made available in more than 400 new locations nationwide. The film comes out on Netflix on December 2, 2026​​​​​, so it will need to keep proving popular over the next few weeks if it wants to contend with the box office hauls of its Oscars rivals.

Both You Can See Everything and La Bola Negra will have to battle with Christopher Nolan's ambitious adaptation of The Odyssey if either wants to come out victorious. Called an "unrivaled cinematic spectacle" in Joe Schmidt's review for Collider, this $1.8 billion box office smash hit is even expected to overtake Ryan Coogler's Sinners as the most nominated film of all time. However, with the aforementioned contenders and other titles continuing to gain momentum, perhaps The Odyssey's journey to victory will be anything but plain sailing.

AMD’s new Venice chips aren’t agentic CPUs, but they’re the best CPUs for agentic AI, says AMD.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Two prominent voices within AMD agree that the company didn’t design its new Zen 6 family, codenamed Venice, explicitly for agentic AI in a clear shot at Nvidia and Arm, who’ve both described their latest data center CPUs as “agentic” in nature. In separate talks, AMD’s chief Zen architect Mike Clark and corporate VP Madhu Rangarajan said that the company didn’t predict the agentic wave when Zen 6 development started.

We just wrapped up testing AMD's Venice CPUs, which you can read more about in our Zen 6 benchmarking article.

CPU development cycles are measured in years, and given that the agentic frenzy around CPUs hit around March of this year, it’s unreasonable to expect that anyone has developed silicon specifically for agentic AI, much less a general-purpose data center CPU. And still, Nvidia claims its Vera CPU was “purpose-built” for agentic AI, while Arm says its AGI CPU was “designed for the demands of agentic AI.”

“We think that the EPYC 9006 CPU is the best CPU for the agentic era, and spoiler alert, we didn’t magically know four years ago that agentic was going to be a thing and purpose-built something for agentic,” said Rangarajan in a session with media. “The reality is an agentic workflow is a diverse set of tasks… and in order to execute those tasks well, you need compute that is able to handle a diverse set of workloads with a full portfolio.”

Introducing the Zen 6 microarchitecture, Mike Clark agreed with the sentiment: “I’m not going to mislead and act like I knew agentic computing was coming, or we knew it, but we did strongly believe that the CPU would always have a place in the software ecosystem of AI… And as agentic [has] blown up, we [are] able to now deploy that for agentic compute workloads.”

As AMD rolls out its next-generation microarchitecture with Venice CPUs, it’s targeting the messaging from Arm and Nvidia, which have posed the most direct threat to AMD’s continued rise in the data center on the back of agentic workloads. And indeed, there are performance vectors where Nvidia’s Vera looks strong for agentic workloads, as you can see in our Nvidia Vera testing. AMD’s argument, however, is that agentic AI calls for a broad range of CPUs just as general-purpose workloads do, pushing back on the single-SKU approach of Nvidia and Arm.

Our testing of two AMD Venice chips supports that argument, as well, with the dense EPYC 9996 with its 256 cores and 512 threads offering peak throughput performance, while the frequency-optimized EPYC 9686F goes toe-to-toe with Vera in the role of an AI host node. AMD is launching two additional ranges next year, with Venice-X targeting HPC with its 3D-stack cache, and Verano going directly after Vera as an AI host node with an LPDDR5X memory system.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Although the workload characteristics of agentic AI are still in the process of being defined, we have a better understanding of how those loops work now than we did at the beginning of the year. And like all complex topics, the answer to what is important in a CPU for agentic AI is the always-disappointing “it depends.”

As we break down in our early testing procedure for agentic AI, the workload characteristics and demand on the CPU/memory are defined by what the agent is doing, and in the data center, what several agents are doing at high concurrency when sandboxed. A coding agent will generally call more tools that are taxing on the CPU than a web browsing agent, for example, while an agent that queries a database may call for strong memory performance.

Of course, there are some vectors of performance that can’t be addressed with different SKUs (such as that strong memory performance). However, the potential diversity of agentic workloads certainly makes an argument for CPU variety. At a most basic level, an agentic loop allows the LLM to understand functions you’ve defined, and then pass arguments to those functions to execute them. We’re talking about a workload as broad as what you can define in a function in languages like Python and Rust.

AMD winds up Zen 6 cores for faster clock speeds

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chips boast up to 256 cores, clocks up to 5.3 GHz, and IPC gains of 20-31 percent, just not all on the same SKU

After teasing its 256-core Epycs for more than a year, AMD is finally ready to talk about architectural changes to the Zen core that not only make it possible, but will eventually power its next generation of Ryzen processors in desktop and mobile PCs.

During a press event in Las Vegas last week, AMD spilled the beans on its latest Zen cores, which promise an average 27 percent uplift in instructions per clock (IPC) across a variety of AI and server workloads, along with boosted clocks ranging from 4.1 to 5.3 GHz. We expect to see even higher frequencies on consumer chips before long, but AMD isn’t quite ready to talk about Ryzen just yet.

CPU performance is a function of power, IPC, and clocks, and with gains across all three, the cores are shaping up to be one of the company’s biggest generational jumps ever.

As in past Epyc launches, Zen 6 isn’t one core. It comes in two flavors: a frequency-optimized variant (Zen 6) and a density-optimized version (Zen 6c).

This split dates back to the launch of AMD’s Bergamo Epycs in 2023, which packed 128 compact Zen 4c cores into just eight chiplets. The chip’s debut drew comparisons to Intel’s still relatively new Efficient (E-cores) for obvious reasons. But under the hood, AMD’s Zen 4c was nothing like what Intel was doing. The core itself was nearly identical to Zen 4; it simply traded clocks and a bit of cache per core for a more densely packed CPU die.

The only difference between running an application on Bergamo or Genoa was how quickly it executed. The same could not be said of Intel’s P and E cores, which used different ISAs.

This remained a defining characteristic and advantage of AMD’s compact core architecture through Zen 5, which allowed it to ship Turin parts with up to 192 cores without users having to worry about whether their app was running on a compact core or not.

With Zen 6c, at least as we understand it, this philosophy is largely unchanged. Both Zen 6 and Zen 6c share the same ISA, with clock being the main differentiator. There are other micro-architectural differences between the two cores designed to balance IPC and clock speeds more effectively.

Looking at the core’s block diagram, this isn’t immediately obvious. Zen 6 and Zen 5 share a very similar front and backend.

We'd be tempted to call the core iterative if it weren't for the 20 to 31 percent IPC uplift for cloud and AI workloads that AMD claims over Zen 5. This suggests that core for core, a 4.1 GHz Zen 6c-based Venice Epyc should deliver performance on par with a Zen 5-based Turin Epyc running at 5 GHz.

That's a pretty sizable jump, but just because AMD is seeing generational performance uplift in enterprise server and cloud workloads doesn't mean we'll see the same gains on the client side. Many will recall the roughly 5 percent performance gains gaming PC enthusiasts reported with the Ryzen 9000-series, leading to the rather unfortunate turn of phrase "Zen 5 percent."

That happened because game engines at the time simply weren't CPU-bottlenecked, and so faster cores could only raise the ceiling so much. Even so, AMD fellow and "Zen daddy" Mike Clark tells El Reg that PC buyers expect more than a 6 percent gain from Zen 6.

We're glossing over a bunch of low-level architectural optimizations here, but according to Clark, one of the biggest drivers of performance was to the front end. Zen 5 introduced a dual front end, which for simple workloads worked great, but lost a lot of steam when confronted with more dynamic loads.

"The Zen 6 team did a really good job for those larger footprint workloads that have a bipolar nature," Clark said. "It's not something that comes through. You're not going to see it in a block diagram very well. It's sort of subtle... but it's a big lever."

Another contributor was the move to a distributed scheduler in the out-of-order pipeline. One of the biggest performance drivers in modern CPU cores is that they don't execute instructions in the order they receive them. Instead, they reorder the instructions so as to keep the core's backend execution units fed with a constant stream of instructions.

This is where we see the biggest difference between Zen 6 and Zen 6c. To account for the slower clocks on the latter core, AMD gave the core's scheduler deeper queues, which Clark tells us helps with higher-latency workloads. Meanwhile, from what we understand, Zen 6's shallower queue enables it to clock higher on less power.

Finally, on the backend, AMD made several subtle changes which should have major implications for vector heavy workloads, including AI. One of the more significant was the ability to perform four AVX2 or two AVX-512 loads per cycle.

While this doesn't improve AVX-512 performance much, for applications that haven't been updated to use it, the core is now able to perform twice as many loads, which should improve throughput for legacy applications. This should come in handy, particularly on PCs, where Intel's outright lack of AVX-512 support has made AVX2 the lowest common denominator for a lot of software.

That's not to say AVX-512 didn't get some love this generation. For those running AI workloads on CPUs, Zen 6 now natively supports FP16 datatypes. Previously FP16 was supported, but required padding the data to FP32, which meant the lower precision didn't buy much in terms of performance. That's no longer the case in Zen 6.

And while we're on the topic of AI acceleration, Clark notes that Zen 6 doubled the VNNI-INT8 pipes from two to four, and should effectively double throughput for workloads that support it.

While not strictly part of the core, AMD's Zen 6 processors also address one of the biggest asymmetries associated with its frequency and density optimized cores.

Going back to Bergamo, opting for a density-optimized Epyc didn't just come at the expense of clocks, but cache too. Each Zen 4c or Zen 5c chiplet contained twice as many cores, but the same amount of L3 cache.

16 channels of DDR5, 5 GHz clock speeds, and support for CXL 3.1 memory god boxes? What's not to like?

With Zen 6 and Zen 6c, you may be giving up some clocks, but cache per core is now a constant 4 MB. As we discussed in a Venice deep dive back in September, each of the processor's eight CPU dies contain 12 or 32 cores and 48 and 128 MB respectively.

The benefit here is that users don't have to worry about different ISAs or cache hierarchies when optimizing their software stacks against AMD's Epyc processors. The same cannot be said of Intel's Xeon processors.

As we alluded to earlier, Chipzilla's performance and efficiency cores use different micro-architectures, ISAs, cache hierarchies, clocks, and even cluster their cores differently. This can pose real headaches if you have workloads that take advantage of fat vector registers like AVX-512, or accelerators like AMX, which will work just fine on Intel's P-cores but won't even run on their E-cores.

This, along with support for SMT (Hyperthreading), which Intel has dropped for now, could help the House of Zen vacuum up even more market share from its long-time rival. ®

Where to Eat During AFM 2026: Century City and Beverly Hills’ Best Restaurants

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

AFM 2026 is in the center of the independent film business dealmaking world and close to many of the entertainment biz’s favorite restaurants. Century City and nearby Beverly Hills are home to the major agencies, law firms and financing entities, which equals power lunching and see-and-be-seen gathering spots close to the confab. Throw in some legendary eateries, and AFMers are perfectly positioned to dine where Hollywood dealmakers, agency foot soldiers and bold names eat out.Century City’s dining options start at the host venue: Birdie G’s is the Fairmont Century Plaza’s new full-service restaurant. Expect classic American cooking in a relaxed setting. There will be mobile coffee stations and daily grab-and-go items near the Fairmont’s lower-level conference rooms. Choices include hearty breakfast burritos, creative salads and an array of sandwiches.At the adjacent Westfield Century City, where AFM badge holders can take advantage of a discount program, more than 65 food outlets populate the multi-level, open-air mall.Specializing in dim sum, Din Tai Fung (10250 Santa Monica Blvd., second level, dtf.com) excels in Taiwan-style, handmade soup dumplings and flavorful noodle dishes. Waits can be extensive, so sit at the bar, reserve ahead or use yelp.com to join the real-time waitlist. Related Stories Pixar Names Jonathan Garson as President; Jim Morris Moves to Emeritus Role

Terra, the restaurant at Eataly Los Angeles (10250 Santa Monica Blvd., Eataly.com), boasts ample outdoor seating to enjoy Terra’s wood-fired Italian dishes and craft cocktails. The Monday-Friday happy hour on the bar terrace features $10 glasses of wine, cocktails and Aperol spritzes paired with grilled appetizers.

On the lower level of the nearby Century Building, Hinoki & the Bird (10 W. Century Dr., hinokiandthebird.com) attracts the high-powered lunch crowd, including CAA agents along with their clients and execs from the Fox studio lot. Serving globally inspired California cuisine, lunch favorites range from sashimi starters to the ever-popular crispy jidori chicken to the kale salad topped with spiced cashews. Enticing dinner entrees include the hinoki-scented black cod and dry-aged Peking duck. The tucked-away location provides privacy; the sheltered, heated outdoor patio is open year-round. Reservations are highly recommended. Monday-Friday happy hour runs 5-7 p.m.

Beverly Hills excels at chic hotel dining. Opened in May, Gemma on the rooftop of the Waldorf Astoria Beverly Hills (9850 Wilshire Blvd., waldorfastoriabeverlyhills.com) has unequaled views across Beverly Hills. The upscale pan-Asian fare (highlights: dim sum, seafood raw bar, lobster burger and Singaporean noodles) and fanciful cocktails more than satisfy the rooftop’s stylish crowd. Edoardo Baldi’s Tuscan steakhouse Baldi (baldi.com) brings the restaurateur’s signature hospitality and famed pasta to the Waldorf’s first floor.

The Maybourne Beverly Hills (225 N. Canon Dr., maybournebeverlyhills.com) boasts a caviar and oyster bar at its cozy Maybourne Bar. Tradition and old-school service reign on the leafy patio of the Beverly Hills Hotel’s Polo Lounge (9641 Sunset Blvd., dorchestercollection.com), a go-to for Hollywood’s elite for decades.

Mastro’s is celebrating 25 years, and the Garden at the Penthouse at Mastro’s Steakhouse Beverly Hills (246 Canon Dr., mastrosrestaurants.com) welcomes power players like Michael B. Jordan, Emily Blunt and John Krasinski to its clubby third-floor indoor-outdoor dining room. For dinner, there are massive steaks and chops and a heady assortment of seafood.

For those seeking a flowery, photo-ready indoor-outdoor space that feels miles away, take the world’s prettiest elevator to the rooftop restaurant Monsieur Dior by Dominique Crenn (323 N. Rodeo Dr., monsieurdiorbeverlyhills.com). The inventive lunch, dinner and lounge menus come from Michelin-starred restaurateur/chef Dominique Crenn, paired with elegant interiors by architect Peter Marino. Sip on a glass of Chateau Minuty, the house rosé wine from Provence, France, to embrace the French ambiance.

The businessperson’s lunch crowd flocks to Avra Beverly Hills (233 N. Beverly Dr., theavragroup.com) for the prix fixe, three-course Mediterranean-style lunch special priced at $41.95. Start with a Greek salad, then move on to the grilled salmon and then finish with a baklava, all served in Avra’s bright, open-air setting populated by the city’s elite.

XRP broke through support at $1.10 and dropped to around $1.04, leaving it less than 5% above the key $1 level. The Relative Strength Index has sunk to 25.8, deep in oversold territory, but buyers have yet to turn the trend. If XRP loses $1, the next major support sits near $0.90. Prices can differ by exchange, with Binance support levels running about $0.10 lower. XRP is now in a 30% correction. Derivatives data adds to the caution, as traders on Binance remain positioned for gains, and past bottoms have usually formed when funding turned deeply negative. That happened this summer, when XRP fell to $0.70 before rebounding 53%. The 30-day funding sum currently stands at +0.18%. Spot XRP ETFs also had one of their worst days of the year, with net outflows of 9.37 million XRP, worth about $10.4 million.

Chelsea boss Xabi Alonso called out for 'worrying' Estêvão Willian decision

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Paul Merson believes Xabi Alonso "played into Sunderland's hands" on Sunday and also criticized the Chelsea manager for not starting Estêvão Willian.

Despite delivering one of his finest displays in a Blues shirt just eight days earlier during the 2-0 victory over Manchester United at Stamford Bridge, Estêvão started on the bench at the Stadium of Light. The Brazilian was introduced at halftime to replace Levi Colwill, who endured a difficult opening 45 minutes up against Black Cats striker Brian Brobbey.

The 19-year-old was unable to turn the tide for the visiting side in the second half, as Sunderland secured a vital three points, via football.london. Lifelong Chelsea supporter Merson feels Alonso got his team selection badly wrong by leaving the teenager out of the starting lineup.

Ryan Reynolds and Rob Mac address Wrexham executive's abrupt exit

USMNT legend Alexi Lalas on Mauricio Pochettino to Man Utd links amidst pressure on Michael Carrick

-Credit:Lee Parker - CameraSport, CameraSport via Getty Images

Writing in his Sky Sports column, the former Arsenal midfielder said: "I thought Xabi Alonso played into Sunderland's hands, if I'm being honest. He went there and showed them a little bit more respect than what I thought he should have.

"They were bottom of the league with five points, and they were bottom of the league with five points for a reason.

"Do you know what really worried me? Estêvão was Chelsea's best player by a million miles last week against Manchester United - especially in the first half when they were really bad. They should have been 2-0 down at half-time.

"But all of a sudden, they go to Sunderland and he's not playing. And he was missed. He's someone who can take the ball up the pitch for them.

"Alonso had to pick Estêvão, play the same team they put out against Man Utd and get him to run with the ball. Instead, he tried to make it tight, and make sure they don't get beat over winning the game."

To listen to the latest episode of 'All Out Soccer' - CLICK HERE

Alonso has handed Estêvão a Premier League start on just one occasion this season, fueling speculation regarding the Brazilian's future at the club — though such talk has somewhat subsided following his appearance against Manchester United.

Chelsea host Leeds United at Stamford Bridge on November 21, their first fixture following the final international break of 2026. Estêvão, meanwhile, has been named in Carlo Ancelotti's latest Brazil squad for upcoming friendlies against Japan and Singapore.

Despite limited playing time at Stamford Bridge, Ancelotti has urged Estêvão to seize his opportunity on the international stage: "We hope he can show all the quality he has. We'll keep an eye on him.

"If he doesn't play a single minute at Chelsea, we'll keep an eye on him until the day we think he's no longer useful for the national team."

Brazil's second friendly, scheduled for November 17 in Singapore at the National Stadium in Kallang, poses a logistical headache for Estêvão, with the roughly 15-hour return flight to London set to significantly disrupt his preparations ahead of the Leeds clash.
